How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Politics is not just about policy-it can become deeply personal, straining relationships between family members, friends, and romantic partners who hold different political views. Extending prior work that mostly focused on romantic relationships in nonrepresentative samples, we tested whether political dissimilarity predicted worse relationship quality across romantic and non-romantic close relationships in a nationally representative U.S. sample (N = 1,075; 45% Republican, 55% Democrat) during a period of heightened political tension around the 2024 U.S. election. In a ten-wave longitudinal study spanning September 2024 to October 2025, greater political dissimilarity predicted worse relationship quality and a 22% higher odds of relationship breakup. These effects were comparable across various sociodemographics and close relationship types, indicating broad generalizability.

These findings underscore the widespread relational costs of political dissimilarity: Political divides spill deeply into personal life, eroding the quality and stability of people's closest ties.

The Political is Relational: Political Dissimilarity Predicts Worse Relationship Quality

Close relationships are among the most important determinants of people's well-being and health (Gómez-López et al., 2019; Pezirkianidis et al., 2023; Thomas et al., 2017). Yet, the people we rely on for connection do not always share our political beliefs. Against a backdrop of increasing political polarization (Böttcher and Gersbach, 2020; Gu and Wang, 2022), politics has become increasingly salient and consequential in everyday life, shaping how people interact with family members (Van Duyn & Pool, 2025), friends (Bohman et al., 2019), and romantic partners (Lodhi et al., 2025). Political differences can introduce conflict into close relationships, and could even contribute to their dissolution (Güngör & Ditto, 2026). Despite widespread concern that politics is driving people apart, we lack a clear empirical understanding of how strongly political differences predict relationship satisfaction, conflict, and dissolution, and whether these outcomes are widespread or concentrated among particular people and relationships.

Political views represent values and moral foundations that are central to people's identities (Strupp-Levitsky et al., 2020). Accordingly, people prefer to form relationships with others who are politically similar, particularly in early romantic relationships (Cotton and Castro, 2025; Huber & Malhotra, 2017; Mallinas et al., 2018). Political similarity is also common in established relationships, including marriages (Watson et al., 2004), friendships (Buliga & MacInnis, 2020), and families (Chen & Rohla, 2018). Among 4,000 participants mostly from the United States, fewer than one in four couples differed in party affiliation, and less than 8% were Republican-Democrat pairs (Gordon et al., 2025). However, these broad categories might obscure subtle political differences: even same-party pairs may differ in the strength of their party identification, ideology, or positions on specific issues, with potential downstream consequences as these differences accumulate through daily interactions.

Prior research has yielded mixed findings regarding the association between political dissimilarity and relationship quality, with results appearing to vary, in part, according to how political dissimilarity was operationalized. Studies using categorical indicators of political differences have reported inconsistent findings. For example, voting differently from a partner was associated with greater conflict and fewer positive relationship behaviours in one study (Afifi et al., 2020), but was unrelated to relationship quality in another (Fangmeier et al., 2020). Similarly, belonging to a different political party from one's partner was weakly or inconsistently linked with relationship quality (Gordon et al., 2025). Another study that assessed newlyweds' categorical agreement or disagreement on a list of political issues found no links with marital satisfaction (Luo & Klohnen, 2005).

In contrast, measures that allow for more nuanced responses, such as subjective measures of perceived political dissimilarity or comparing self and other's continuous ratings of political orientation, have demonstrated more consistent effects. Perceived political dissimilarity predicted poorer romantic relationship quality cross-sectionally and across a 14day daily diary period (Gordon et al., 2025). Similarly, a response surface analysis examining the degree of mismatch between partners' political orientations found that greater dissimilarity was linked with poorer relationship quality in 41,606 individuals from 74 countries (Kowal, 2026). Although research beyond romantic relationships remains limited, one study of parent-child relationships similarly found that the degree of dissimilarity in political perspectives predicted poorer relationship quality (Yu et al., 2024). Together, these findings suggest that capturing the degree of political dissimilarity, rather than coarse categorical distinctions, may be important for understanding its relational consequences.

Together, these findings suggest that the relational significance of political differences may depend on how those differences are conceptualized and measured. Categorical indicators may obscure meaningful variation in the degree of political dissimilarity, whereas perceived dissimilarity may capture the subjective significance of these differences. Yet existing studies have rarely examined distinct dimensions of political dissimilarity within the same investigation. Moreover, much of the literature has focused on romantic relationships and relied on samples disproportionately composed of White and politically liberal participants.

Research has also rarely examined relational outcomes longitudinally, leaving open whether political dissimilarity predicts not only dissatisfaction and conflict but also the eventual dissolution of relationships. These limitations make it difficult to establish the magnitude, scope, and generalizability of the relational costs of political differences.

Present Study

To address limitations of the existing studies, the present study examines political dissimilarity in close relationships using a nationally representative longitudinal design spanning a highly polarized period in recent American history. We tracked participants on Prolific across one year around the 2024 U.S. presidential election, from the height of campaign season through the post-inauguration period. We focused on three research questions: First, how common is political dissimilarity among romantic and non-romantic close relationships? Second, does political dissimilarity predict worse relationship quality?

Third, does the association between political similarity and relationship quality depend on characteristics of the relationships (e.g., length, closeness), individuals (e.g., age, gender, race, political perceptions), and the political context (i.e., pre-vs. post-election)?

The present research has several unique strengths. First, given that many people are not in romantic relationships (Fry, 2025) and may have political differences with all close others (Güngör & Ditto, 2026), we assessed political dissimilarity across both romantic and non-romantic close others (e.g., family, friends) and tested potential moderation by relationship type. Second, we operationalized dissimilarity in three ways, including perceived political similarity, degree of mismatch between self and other political ideology, and degree of mismatch between self and other party alignment. Using multilevel Dyadic Response Surface Analysis (DRSA), we capture a more granular assessment of the degree of mismatch between self and other perceptions, rather than coarse cross-party versus same-party comparisons. Third, beyond subjective relationship quality perceptions, we assessed the ending of close relationships over time (breakups) as an objective behavioural outcome.

Finally, whereas prior longitudinal work has relied on a shorter time span using daily diary designs (e.g., 14 days; Gordon et al., 2025; Yu et al., 2024), the present study tracked participants across a year longitudinally and examined whether effects persist over a substantially longer time horizon.

Research Transparency Statement

General

Study Disclosures

Preregistration: The hypotheses, methods, and data analysis were preregistered (https://osf.io/f6p5w/?view_only=eb2c2432d6244713b369646752fdf651) prior to data analysis. Materials: All study materials are publicly available in the supplement. Data: All primary data are publicly available (https://osf.io/y36xe/overview?view_only=baf1dc535a3d438fa4ca4ad57db00f54). Analysis scripts: All analysis scripts are publicly available (https://osf.io/y36xe/overview? view_only=baf1dc535a3d438fa4ca4ad57db00f54).

Methods

Participants & Procedures

This study is part of a larger political longitudinal study examining political beliefs, emotions, and well-being around the 2024 United States election. In September 2024 (T0), we recruited a nationally representative sample of 1,075 individuals from the United States through Prolific Academic. Participants matched the distributions of age, sex, race/ethnicity, and political alignment of the 2020 United States Census (see Table 1). Data were collected between September 2024 and June 2025. To preserve the representativeness of the initial sample, all participants were invited to complete three bi-weekly surveys leading up to the election on November 5, 2024 (T1, T2, T3) and three bi-weekly surveys following the election (T4, T5, T6). Participants also completed an additional survey (T7) after inauguration in January 2025, a six-month follow-up survey in June 2025 (T8), and a oneyear follow-up survey after the election in October 2025 (T9). See Table 1 for sociodemographic information. See Table 2 for Sample size information. Multiple data quality checks were employed, see the supplement for more details.

To be eligible for the study, participants had to be Prolific users in the United States, be at least 18 years old, and be willing to participate in a multi-week longitudinal study.

Surveys at each time point took approximately 20 minutes to complete, for which participants were compensated approximately USD $3.50. Data quality was ensured through multiple means. See supplementary materials for more details and Figure 1 for a visualization of the study's procedural timeline. At T0, participants answered questions either about a romantic partner if they were in a committed romantic relationship, or if they were not in a committed relationship, they answered questions about a non-romantic close other. At each timepoint after T0 (T1-T9), participants were prompted to indicate whether they were still in contact with the partner or close other they previously identified (i.e., participants had provided the initials for this person at T0). Participants with a romantic partner were asked "Are you still in a romantic relationship with this person?" ; Participants with a non-romantic close other were asked "Do you still have contact with this person (i.e., interacting at least a few times per month)?" For both questions, breakup was coded 0 (yes) and 1 (no). All analyses used data on the relationship that participants selected at T0.

Figure 1

Procedural Timeline Note. Each time point is accompanied by its respective sample size and the measurements taken.

Among the participants reporting on a romantic partner (61%), the majority were married (42%), with the remainder dating exclusively (14%), engaged (2%), in a commonlaw relationship (2%), or dating others in addition to the selected partner (1%). Among those who reported on a non-romantic close other (38%), the most common relationship types were best friend (9%), mother or father (7%), sibling (6%), friend (6%), and other family member (5%), with less common types including roommate (1%), co-worker (1%), acquaintance (<1%), or other (2%). All relationships were rated as relatively close (M = 5.12, SD = 1.16) from 0 (Not at all) to 6 (Extremely). Final sample Size 1031 894 838 803 799 794 747 694 621 554 Final N for romantic relationships 633 534 504 496 480 478 453 418 369 339 Final N for non-romantic relationships 398 360 334 307 319 316 294 276 252 215

Note. Across the whole study (T1-T9), 96 people had at least one breakup (breakup rate: 9.3%; Romantic: 6.5%, Non-Romantic: 13.6%).

At T0, to assess political dissimilarity, participants completed three different measurements of political dissimilarity, including perceived political dissimilarity, political ideology (for self and close other), and party alignment (for self and close other). Also at T0, participants reported on sociodemographics (e.g., age, gender, race), relationship characteristics (e.g., length, closeness), and political perceptions (e.g., perceived importance of political similarity) as potential moderators. At each subsequent time point (T1 to T9), participants reported on outcome variables, including relationship conflict, relationship satisfaction, and whether they broke up with their initially-selected close other (i.e., whether participants lost contact with the close other they identified at T0). If participants had broken up with the close other they identified at T0, they were asked to identify a new close other to report on in subsequent surveys to keep the survey length equal across participants. All analyses used data specific to the first close other that participants identified at T0.

Measures

Political dissimilarity.

Three operationalizations of political dissimilarity were assessed at T0. We focused on perceived political dissimilarity, measured with a single item: "How similar or different are your and your partner's [this person's 1 ] political beliefs?" Responses were rated from 0 (completely different) to 6 (completely similar). Scores were reverse-keyed so that higher scores indicate higher dissimilarity.

In addition, we assessed participants' perceptions of self and other political ideology and party alignment. This approach has been used in prior studies on political congruence (e.g., Yu et al., 2024), and is viable according to dyadic evidence. Specifically, Gordon et al. (2025) found that participants' perceptions of their partners' political ideology were very highly correlated with partners' own reports of their ideology (r = .86), and combined these two measures in their analyses. Thus, participants are likely able to estimate close others' political views accurately. Moreover, subjective perceptions in the literature have been more consistently associated with relationship outcomes, rather than objective matching between political views measured in dyads.

Dissimilarity in political ideology was assessed via the mismatch between ratings for one's own political ideology and their close other's political ideology. Participants reported their own political ideology by answering the question, "If you had to choose, would you consider yourself a liberal or a conservative?", and reported their perceptions of a romantic partner's or close other's political ideology by answering the question, "Would you consider your partner [this person] a liberal or a conservative?", both on a scale of -3 (very liberal) to 3 (very conservative).

1 Item wording for participants varied based on whether they were reporting on a romantic partner or (nonromantic) close other. For romantic partners, participants were asked about the "partner." For close others, participants were asked about "this person."

Dissimilarity in political alignment was assessed via the mismatch between ratings for one's own political party alignment and their close other's party alignment. Participants reported their own party alignment by answering the question, "Do you think of yourself as closer to the Republican Party or to the Democratic Party?", and reported party alignment of the close other by answering the question, "Do you think of your partner/this person as closer to the Republican Party or to the Democratic Party?", both on a scale of -3 (much closer to the Democratic Party) to 3 (much closer to the Republican Party), with 0 labelled "neither".

Recent Relationship Quality

At each time point from T1 to T9, participants rated relationship satisfaction on a scale from 0 (Never) to 6 (Extremely): "Over the past 7 days, how satisfied have you been with the relationship you have with your partner [this person]?" Participants also rated relationship conflict on a scale from 0 (Never) to 6 (All the time): "Over the past 7 days, how often did you argue or experience tension in your relationship with your partner [this person]?" These outcome variables are non-redundant indicators of relationship quality (i.e., correlated at r = -.50 between-person and r = -.23 within-person) and were reliably assessed over time (ICCs = .67 and .59 for satisfaction and conflict, respectively).

Breakup

At each timepoint from T1 to T9, we assessed if participants had broken up with the person they initially chose to report on at T0. Participants with romantic partners were asked, "When you entered this study, you identified your romantic partner: [partner initials].

Are you still in a romantic relationship with this person?" Participants reporting on close others were asked, "When you entered this study, you identified someone you feel close to: [close other initials]. Do you still have contact with this person (i.e., interacting at least a few times per month)?" Participants were asked to select 1 (yes) or 0 (no), with "yes" indicating no breakup and "no" indicating a breakup. Please refer to Table 2 for a breakdown of the study's romantic and non-romantic relationship sample sizes. Breakup rates were below 4% at any given time point and 9.3% across the full study.

Non-romantic close others were more likely to break up (13.6%) compared to romantic partners (6.5%). These breakup rates were normative based on previous research (Rosenfeld & Thomas, 2012).

Moderators

Several moderators were assessed to examine the generalizability of the associations between perceived political (dis)similarity and relationship quality outcomes: Sociodemographic characteristics (age, gender, race, income, education), relationship characteristics (relationship type, general closeness, and length), individual differences in political perceptions (political growth mindset, perceived importance of political similarity, general political conversation frequency, and time spent on political discussion and the election context (comparing the pre-election period (T1-T3) from the post-election period (T4-T9).

Individual Sociodemographics.

Participants were asked to respond to several singleitem questions regarding their age (18 to 100 or older), gender (man, woman, non-binary, other, or prefer not to say), race (Black, White, Middle Eastern, Latino, East Asian, Southeast Asian, South Asian, Native American, or other), income (9 ordinal categories from Less than $15,000 to greater than $200,000), and highest level of education achieved (7 ordinal categories from less than seventh grade to postgraduate degree). See "Participants" section for distributions of these variables.

Relationship Characteristics. Relationship type was operationalized at T0 based on participants' current relationship status. Participants who selected options stating that they were in an exclusive, non-exclusive, common-law, engaged, or married relationship were classified as having a romantic partner. Conversely, participants who reported being single, casually dating, or selecting "other"foot_0 for their relationship status were classified as having a non-romantic close other.

General closeness

was assessed with a single item asking, "In general, how close has your relationship with your partner/this person felt?" Responses were scored on a 7-point scale ranging from 0 (not at all) to 6 (extremely), with a midpoint of 3 (moderately). The sample reported high initial relationship closeness at T0 (M = 5.13, SD = 1.15).

Relationship length was determined by asking participants how many years and additional months they had known the identified close other. These values were then converted into a continuous variable representing total relationship length in years (calculated as years + months/exp12). Across the entire sample at baseline, the average relationship length was 20.60 years (SD = 14.22)foot_1 . Participants reporting on a non-romantic close other (e.g., family members, long-term friends) had known that individual for an average of 24.67 years (SD = 18.12). Participants reporting on a romantic partner at baseline yielded a mean of 20.26 years (SD = 13.79).

Individual Differences in Political Perceptions.

We assessed several individual differences in people's beliefs about politics. First, at T0, participants were asked about their political growth mindset and asked to respond to two statements: "People's political views can change over time" and "People can change their political views if they want to." Items were averaged into a composite (α = .77) and both rated from 0 (Strongly disagree) to 6 (Strongly agree). Participants reported a high political growth mindset on average, with a mean score of 5.05 (SD = 0.99) on the 0 to 6 scale.

At T0, participants were asked how often they engaged in political conversations with their romantic partners or non-romantic close others on a scale from 0 (Never or almost never) to 6 (Multiple times a day). General political conversation frequency at baseline was distributed around the scale midpoint (Once a week; M = 3.09, SD = 1.76).

To operationalize the frequency of political discussion more objectively, at each timepoint between T1 and T9, participants were asked how much time they spent discussing politics with their romantic partners or close others in the past week, calculated using hours and minutes. Across all timepoints, participants reported an average political discussion time of 0.95 hours (SD = 1.87) per week. This variable varied across timepoints (ICC = .47) and thus decomposed into between-person and within-person components, which were tested together as moderators.

Finally, at each timepoint from T1 to T9, participants rated their perceived importance of political similarity. Participants were asked, "How important is it to you that your partner and you share similar political views?" Responses ranged from 0 (Not at all important) to 6 (Extremely important). Across time points, participants perceived sharing similar political views to be moderately important (M = 3.31, SD = 2.08). Perceived importance mostly varied between-person (ICC = .79), and we focused on testing it as a between-person moderator.

Pre-vs. Post-Election Context.

Given that the context before versus after the election may be different in levels of stressfulness, controllability, and certainty, we included election context as a potential moderator. Data obtained before the election were coded as 0 (i.e., T0 to T3) and data obtained after the election were coded as 1 (i.e., T4 to T9).

Covariates

We also assessed participants' own well-being at each timepoint (T0-T9) using seven items, rated from 0 (Not at all) to 6 (Extremely). Participants read the instruction, "Please tell us how you have been feeling over the past week." Items were drawn from established measures of life satisfaction (Diener et al., 1985) and psychological well-being (Ryff & Keyes, 1995), including "I felt satisfied with life", "I felt like my life had a clear sense of purpose", and "I felt like I enjoyed life" (αs: .92-.93). These items were each averaged into a composite score. These variables were relatively stable across time (ICC = .79; M = 3.77, SD = 1.42). Well-being was correlated with greater relationship satisfaction (r = .38) and lower relationship conflict (r = -.26) at the between-person level. Correlations between well-being and relationship quality outcomes were much weaker at the within-person level (rs = .11 and -.09, respectively).

Analytical Strategy

Each of the three operationalizations of political dissimilarity was examined in a separate set of pre-registered models. When considering participants' single ratings of perceived political dissimilarity, we used multilevel modelling (MLM) to examine whether dissimilarity at baseline (T0; Level-2) predicted relationship satisfaction and conflict across the study period (T1-T9; Level-1). All models included a random intercept for participants and no random slopes, with repeated measures of relationship quality outcomes nested within participants. We also controlled for participants' well-being at each time point when testing the associations between political dissimilarity and relationship quality. This allows us to rule out the alternative explanation that people who were doing worse in general perceive lower political similarity with their close other and also have worse relationships.

When considering the mismatch between participants' separate ratings of their own versus their close other's ideology (or party alignment), we used Multilevel Response Surface Analysis (RSA), which extends standard RSA to account for the nested structure of repeated outcome measurements (Nestler et al., 2019). To examine dissimilarity effects, ratings for both self and other are entered simultaneously as Level-2 predictors along with their squared terms and cross-product. For each participant, self and other ratings were centered using a pooled average. Repeated measures of relationship quality outcomes were nested within participants. Significant incongruence effects indicate significant effects of dissimilarity. This approach allowed us to test both the direction and magnitude of any mismatch (e.g., whether a liberal participant paired with a conservative close other differs from a conservative participant paired with a liberal close other), which cannot be tested using simple difference scores. Models were estimated using restricted maximum likelihood (REML) via the function lmer() from the "lme4" package, with a random intercept for participants.

In exploratory analysis predicting breakups, we used logistic regression to predict whether a participant was more likely to experience at least one breakup across the study (9.3% overall) if they reported being more politically dissimilar to their close other.

To examine potential moderators in the associations between political dissimilarity and relationship quality outcomes, we tested multiple pre-registered moderators in the models. Level-2 moderators include sociodemographic characteristics (i.e., age, gender, race, income, education), relationship type (i.e., romantic vs. non-romantic), closeness, length, and political growth mindset, which were all assessed at T0. Level-1 moderators include perceived importance of political similarity, political conversation frequency, political discussion time, and the election context (a dummy variable with T1-T3 coded as 0 and T4-T9 coded as 1). We decomposed time-varying moderators (level-1) into person means and person-centered scores and examined both components in the same model. We applied the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure to control the false discovery rate across all moderator tests within each operationalization of dissimilarity.

Results

How Common is Political Dissimilarity in Close Relationships?

How Common is Political Dissimilarity in Close Relationships?

Perceived Similarity

When asked directly about the political similarity between themselves and their close other, most participants reported modest levels of dissimilarity (M = 1.59, SD = 1.49, on a scale). There was also substantial individual variation in perceptions, with 22% of the sample scoring at the scale midpoint or higher on dissimilarity.

Self and Other Ideology and Party Alignment

When asked separately about their own ideology and their close other's ideology, we observe similar patterns: the average absolute difference between these two ratings was 0.91 points (SD = 1.20), where half the sample (51%) gave a different ideology rating for themselves vs. their close other, and 22% of the sample differed from their close other by two or more points. When asked separately about their own party alignment and their close other's party alignment, the average absolute difference between these two ratings was 1.01 points (SD = 1.39), where more than a third of the sample (38%) reported a different level of party alignment for themselves vs. their close other, and 14% of the sample differed from their close other by two or more points.

Existing research has mostly focused on whether people identify as being in the same (vs. different) political party categorically, neglecting potential variance in the strength of participants' perceived dissimilarity, as well as the degree of incongruence between continuous ratings of ideology or party alignment of self and other. People may be registered within the same party but endorse different degrees of party alignment or different ideological leanings. Indeed, even though 62% of the participants reported being registered as the same party as their close other, the same-party dyads contain a highly heterogeneous mix: 12% still reported relatively high perceived dissimilarity (ratings at or above the scale midpoint), 23% showed large ideological differences (more than two points apart), and 30% showed larger party alignment differences (more than two points apart).

Next, we examine whether the three continuous measures of political dissimilarity predict relationship quality.

Does Political Dissimilarity Predict Relationship Quality?

Does Political Dissimilarity Predict Relationship Quality?

Multilevel Models Using Perceived Dissimilarity

First, we ran pre-registered multilevel models to examine whether participants' continuous ratings of perceived political dissimilarity at baseline (T0) predicted relationship satisfaction and conflict at subsequent timepoints (T1-T9). Participants who perceived their relationships to be more dissimilar at baseline reported lower relationship satisfaction across the study, b = -0.23, t(958) = -8.95, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.28, -0.18]. We found comparable effects when examining relationship conflict: Participants who perceived their relationships to be more dissimilar at T0 reported higher relationship conflict across T1-T9, b = 0.06, t(958) = 2.76, p = .006, 95% CI [0.02, 0.09].

The associations between perceived political dissimilarity and satisfaction also held in exploratory robustness checks: when predicting T1-T9 satisfaction controlling for participants' concurrent well-being at each timepoint (T1-T9), b = -0.21, t(958) = -8.71, p < .001, and when we predicted T2-T9 relationship satisfaction when controlling for T1 satisfaction, b = -0.10, t(823) = -5.59, p < .001. The same associations with conflict also held: when predicting T0-T9 conflict controlling for concurrent well-being at each timepoint, b = 0.04, t(958) = 2.22, p = .03, and when predicting T2-T9 conflict controlling for T1 conflict, b = 0.03, t(823) = 1.99, p = .048. These findings indicate that dissimilarity is linked with poorer relationship outcomes even when accounting for participants' concurrent wellbeing and their earlier relationship outcomes.

Multilevel Response Surface Analysis Using Self and Other Ratings

Second, we examined whether political dissimilarity, operationalized as incongruence between participants' own ideology (or party alignment) and their close other's ideology (or party alignment), predicted subsequent relationship quality. Incongruence effects can be tested using multilevel Response Surface Analysis (multilevel RSA; Nestler et al., 2019), which extends standard RSA to account for nesting of repeated measures within participants.

Table S1 (supplement) summarizes the surface parameters that are components of the broader response surface and should be interpreted together rather than in isolation (See Table S2 for underlying polynomial regression coefficients). Figure 2 presents the effects of incongruence visually in a three-dimensional space.

Dissimilarity effects. As shown on the left side of Figure 2, greater incongruence between participants' and their close others' ideology (i.e., greater political dissimilarity) predicted lower relationship satisfaction (a₄ = -0.18, p < .001, Panel A) and higher conflict (a₄ = 0.07, p = .009, Panel C).

Replicating this pattern, as seen on the right side of Figure 2, greater incongruence between participants' and their close others' party alignment also predicted lower satisfaction (a₄ = -0.14, p < .001, panel B) and higher conflict (a₄ = 0.05, p = .03, Panel D). These associations suggest that people who are more politically dissimilar to their close others, both in political ideology and party alignment, reported worse relationship quality outcomes. Response Surface Analysis Plots of Political Ideology (Left) and Party Alignment (Right) Incongruence Predicting Relationship Satisfaction (Top) and Conflict (Bottom)

Additional findings. In addition to general dissimilarity effects, we found some evidence of party asymmetries and extremity effects. In predicting relationship satisfaction, the effects of ideology dissimilarity and party dissimilarity depended on the direction of mismatch (i.e., whether self is more conservative or liberal than the close other). Specifically, dissimilarity mattered more for more conservative participants (relative to their close others):

Satisfaction was particularly low when participants perceived themselves to be more conservative (a3 = -0.18, p < .001) or closer to the Republican party (a3 = -0.12, p = .005) than their close others, compared to when participants are more liberal or closer to the Democratic party than their close others. These effects were not significant in predicting conflict whether we examined ideology (a3 = 0.07, p = .09) or party alignment (a3 = 0.06, p = .08).

In predicting relationship conflict, ideology dissimilarity predicted worse outcomes regardless of the direction of mismatch or whether pairs lean more conservative or more liberal (significant a4 only; Figure 2, Panel C). Party dissimilarity also predicted higher conflict, but the level at which participants matched mattered (significant a4, non-significant a3 and a5). Specifically, pairs where both people are closer to the Republican party had higher conflict than pairs where both people are closer to the Democratic party (a1 = 0.06, p = .002).

This effect was in the same direction, but only marginally significant for ideology predicting conflict, and was not significant for either ideology or party predicting relationship satisfaction (non-significant a1's).

Finally, we found extremity effects specifically for ideology predicting relationship satisfaction, such that pairs who shared more extreme ideologies (whether conservative or liberal) had higher satisfaction than pairs who share moderate ideologies (neither conservative nor liberal) (a2 = 0.04, p < .005). This effect was not significant for party predicting satisfaction, or for either ideology or party predicting conflict.

Logistic Regression Models Predicting Breakups

Across T1-T9 which spanned September 2024 to October 2025, 96 participants (9.3%) experienced at least one breakup with a partner or close other, providing an opportunity to examine dissimilarity as a predictor of breaking up. As pre-registered, we tested this question using logistic regression, considering whether they broke up or not over the course of the study. Greater perceived political dissimilarity predicted higher odds of breakup, b = 0.20, Z(1028) = 3.10, p = .002, OR = 1.22, 95% CI [1.08, 1.39]. Each one-unit increase in perceived political dissimilarity was associated with a 22% increase in the odds of breakup. As can be seen in Table S3 (supplement), participants who differed from their close other in ideology (or party alignment) by 3 or 4 points were almost twice as likely to break up (15%-22%), compared to those who differed from their close other by less than 2 points (7%-10%).

The effect of dissimilarity on breakup was not moderated by participants' own ideology (b = 0.04, z = 1.17, p = .243), participants' party alignment (b = 0.02, z = 0.60, p = .547), or the party participants were registered with (b = -0.002, z = -0.03, p = .979). This effect was also not moderated by the close other's ideology (b = -0.01, z = -0.36, p = .721), close other's party alignment (b = -0.004, z = -0.15, p = .884), or close other's registered party (b = -0.04, z = -0.87, p = .383), suggesting that people who perceived their close other to be more politically dissimilar were more likely to break up with them, regardless of the partisan direction of participants or their close others.

Does Political Dissimilarity Predict Relationship Quality Differently Across Individuals, Relationships, and Contexts?

To examine potential moderators of the association between perceived political dissimilarity (T0) and relationship quality (T1-T9), we included an interaction term in each model. The pre-registered moderators include characteristics of individuals (i.e., sociodemographic variables, political growth mindset, perceived importance of political similarity), relationships (i.e., romantic vs. non-romantic type, length, and closeness, political discussion frequency and political discussion time), and the election context (i.e., pre-vs.

post-election). Each moderator was tested separately and time-varying moderators (i.e., perceived importance of political similarity and political discussion time) were decomposed into between-and within-person components. To account for the number of tests, we corrected for multiple comparisons using the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure. Based on post-hoc power analyses for these moderations (see SOM for more details), the study was adequately powered to detect interaction effects of r ≥ .10 for most moderators, but small effects in the r = .02-.05 range were likely undetected.

We found no consistent evidence that the associations between perceived political dissimilarity and either relationship satisfaction or conflict were significantly moderated by any of the tested moderators. One moderation was significant, but only when predicting relationship conflict and not satisfaction: general relationship closeness significantly attenuated the association between perceived political dissimilarity and greater relationship conflict. Full moderation statistics are reported in the supplement (Table S4).

Taken together, the associations between political dissimilarity and relationship quality outcomes are largely robust and generalizable across sociodemographic groups, relationship types and characteristics, individual differences in political attitudes and engagement, and the broader electoral context.

Discussion

In the current investigation, we found that people who perceived themselves to be more politically dissimilar to their close others (romantic partners, family, friends, etc.) reported worse relationship satisfaction and greater conflict over time.

These findings were based on a nationally representative sample and robust across multiple operationalizations of political dissimilarity and relationship quality. Dissimilarity effects even extended beyond relationship perceptions to relationship stability: people who perceived high dissimilarity from their close others were twice as likely to break up over the next year compared to people who were modestly dissimilar from their close others.

This work has several significant contributions. First, prior research has nearly exclusively focused on romantic relationships, leaving other types of close relationships relatively unexamined. We considered any close relationship (romantic partners, friends, family) and found that dissimilarity effects generalized across these various types of close relationships. This consistency is notable given that these relationships may be characterized by different levels of interdependence, power dynamics, and ease of relationship dissolution, all of which may shape how people perceive and experience political dissimilarity.

Interestingly, participants perceived romantic partners as less politically dissimilar than friends or family members (see supplement), suggesting that non-romantic relationships may be particularly important contexts in which political differences are encountered. Yet despite these differences in the levels of perceived dissimilarity, its associations with relationship quality were consistent across relationship types.

Second, prior research has relied heavily on nonrepresentative samples, limiting our understanding of how broadly the relational costs of political dissimilarity extend across the population. By recruiting a nationally-representative U.S. sample, we were able to examine these associations across a diverse range of individuals and relationships. We also tested whether these associations varied across sociodemographic characteristics, relationship types and lengths, election periods, and politics-related individual differences. We found little evidence of systematic moderation across these factors, suggesting that the relational costs of political dissimilarity are relatively generalizable.

In particular, perceived dissimilarity was associated with poorer relationship quality even among people for whom political similarity was less explicitly important, or who do not often discuss politics. These dissimilarity effects also were not buffered by having a growth mindset that political views can become aligned over time. This suggests that political dissimilarity may operate through broader relational processes beyond conscious preferences for political agreement, such as shared reality (Higgins et al., 2021) or perceived responsiveness and mutual understanding (Itzchakov et al., 2022). For example, people may find it difficult to share the same thoughts and feelings with a politically dissimilar close other or may not feel easily understood by them, due to their different perspectives. Indeed, relationship closeness was a significant buffering factorthe only significant moderator we observed: People who felt closer to their dissimilar close others reported less conflict than those who felt less close to their dissimilar close others. This replicates prior findings that appreciating one's partner and taking their perspectives can buffer the relational costs of political dissimilarity (Gordon et al., 2025).

Third, existing research often operationalized dissimilarity using a single or categorical measure (e.g., same-vs. cross-party). This approach might have neglected important within-party differences between close others, underestimating the prevalence and impact of dissimilarity. We used continuous measures of ideology and party alignment, which captured more fine-grained variation. These continuous ratings were analyzed using

Response Surface Analysis, a uniquely informative tool for examining the degree to which matching vs. mismatch predicts downstream outcomes. This approach is a methodological strength because people who share the same party identity can still perceive themselves as politically dissimilar to varying degrees, as shown in our data. We found converging patterns for both perceived dissimilarity and mismatch between ideology or party alignment ratings, suggesting that subjective perceptions can reflect meaningful psychological differences.

Fourth, by assessing dissimilarity more granularly and leveraging Response Surface Analysis, we are also able to examine for whom political dissimilarity matters most. Greater incongruence in either ideology and party alignment was associated with lower satisfaction and greater conflict, but the direction of mismatch also mattered for satisfaction: participants reported particularly lower satisfaction when they perceived themselves as more conservative than their close other, compared to when they perceived their close other to be more conservative than themselves. In addition, matching was particularly beneficial for participants who had more extreme (vs. moderate) ideologies, predicting the highest satisfaction. This pattern only emerged for ideology, but not party alignment, perhaps because ideologies represent more personal socio-political values (Jost et al., 2009), whereas more variation exists within the same party (Dias et al., 2025).

This study has several limitations that raise exciting directions for future research.

First, even though the present data are longitudinal and we conducted prospective analyses controlling for baseline levels of relationship quality, these data do not provide conclusive causal evidence. Future experiments could manipulate the salience of political dissimilarity, and examine whether focusing on differences worsens momentary relationship perceptions.

Second, the present nationally-representative sample focused on the U.S. and these data cannot address generalizability to other countries with different political structures and norms around political disagreement. Third, even though the sample was large to detect medium moderation effects, we did not have sufficient power to detect smaller moderation effects such as comparing specific non-romantic relationships (friends vs. family). Future studies should extend dissimilarity effects to other cultures and examine whether the same process unfolds across all relationships, directly testing potential mechanisms such as shared reality (Stern and Ondish, 2018) and how people navigate political conflicts (Morey et al., 2012).

Conclusion

Together, the present findings suggest the political is relational-People's perceived political differences from their close others have implications for the quality and stability of their relationships. Across multiple measures of political dissimilarity, multiple relationship quality indicators, and both romantic and non-romantic relationships, people who perceived greater politically dissimilarity in their close relationships reported poorer relationship quality and a greater likelihood of breakup. By extending these patterns across multiple types of individuals, sociodemographic groups, and relationship contexts, this work highlights how political divides spill deeply into people's personal and social lives.

Post-hoc Power Analysis

Post-hoc power analysis indicated that for between-person moderators (i.e., (Ns around 960), our sample had 75% power to detect an interaction effect of r = .10, but only 22% power to detect r = .05. For within-person moderators (i.e., around 6465 observations), power was similar for perceived importance of political similarity, which was similar to a between-person moderator due to high nesting within participant (ICC = .79), and power was somewhat higher (92% at r = .10, 34% at r = .05) for time spent on political discussion (ICC = .47). Power was the highest to detect moderation by election context, which had full within-person variance, i.e., 85% power to detect a small effect of r = .05. Taken together, the study was adequately powered to detect interaction effects of r ≥ .10 for most moderators, but small effects in the r = .02-.05 range were likely undetected. We tested if the absolute differences between self and other ratings of political ideology or party alignment also predicted likelihood of breakup. Greater ideological difference between participants and their close others predicted higher odds of breakup, b = 0.17, z = 2.07, p = .04, OR = 1.18, 95% CI [1.00, 1.37]. Similarly, greater party alignment difference predicted higher odds of breakup, b = 0.19, z = 2.46, p = .01, OR = 1.21, 95% CI [1.03, 1.40].

Each one-point increase in ideological difference (e.g., from 0 points apart to 1 point apart) was associated with an 18% increase in the odds of breakup (OR = 1.18). Two points apart (e.g., one "slightly liberal" and one "slightly conservative") would be associated with a 39% increase. Each one-point increase in party alignment difference was associated with a 21% increase in the odds of breakup (OR = 1.21). Two points apart (e.g., one is "Slightly closer to the Democratic Party", the other is "Slightly closer to the Republican Party") was associated with a 46% increase.

Response Surface Analysis Using Party/Ideology Ratings to Predict Breakup

We also examined whether political dissimilarity, operationalized as incongruence between participants' own ideology (or party alignment) and their perceived close other's ideology (or party alignment), predicted whether participants experienced a breakup across T0-T9. Because breakup was measured at the participant level rather than repeatedly across time, these analyses used logistic Response Surface Analysis rather than multilevel RSA.

Dissimilarity effects.

Unlike the findings for relationship satisfaction and conflict, greater incongruence between participants' and their perceived close others' ideology did not significantly predict breakup (a₄ = 0.04, p = .63). Similarly, incongruence in party alignment was not significantly associated with breakup (a₄ = 0.07, p = .24). Thus, the RSA models did not provide significant evidence that greater mismatch in ideology or party alignment was associated with a greater likelihood of relationship dissolution.

Additional findings. Dissimilarity effects on breakup likelihood did not depend on the direction of the mismatch for either ideology (a₃ = 0.22, p = .101) or party alignment (a₃ = 0.07, p = .53). However, replicating what we found for ideology predicting relationship satisfaction, breakup likelihood was lower when pairs matched on more extreme ideologies (a₂ = -0.09, p = .02) or more extreme party alignment (a₂ = -0.10, p = .04), whether the pairs were more conservative or liberal. There was no matching effect for either ideology (a₁ = 0.05, p = .44) or party alignment (a₁ = 0.08, p = .26), suggesting that matched conservative pairs and matched liberal pairs were similarly likely to breakup.

Measures Ensuring High Data Quality

To be eligible to complete the first survey at baseline, participants were required to demonstrate response integrity by responding to an open-ended prompt asking them to briefly describe their "experiences participating in online surveys." These responses were rated from 0-10 using an AI-based screening tool (Costello et al., 2024). We screened out low-quality or non-engaged participants by excluding those who received a rating of 3 or lower for the coherence or quality of their response. We did not implement a strict filter for excluding AIgenerated responses from real participants. In subsequent waves, to reduce responses from bots or ineligible participants, we only allowed participation from previously-enrolled participants who had completed the initial study at baseline through a custom allow list. We also recorded Prolific IDs, study IDs, and session ID as embedded data. In Qualtrics, we prevented multiple submissions and prevented the survey from showing up in search engine results.

At each time point, two embedded attention checks (e.g., "Please select 'Very much'") were included, and at least 95.6% of the sample passed both attention checks. A total of 1,075 participants passed the attention checks for at least one time point, and were included for final data analysis. The T0 sample size used for analysis was smaller (1,031) because some participants were excluded at T0 for not passing T0 attention checks, but successfully passed them at later time points.

We created a dummy variable to code all data from T1-T3 as 0, and all data from T4-T9 as 1, to determine if the election acted as a moderator between our predictor and outcome variables.

incongruence predicted lower satisfaction (a₄ = -0.14, p < .001) and higher conflict (a₄ = 0.05, p = .031). Thus, across both ideology and party alignment, greater political mismatch was associated with worse relationship quality.

Additional findings. For relationship satisfaction, the effect of political mismatch also depended on its direction. Satisfaction was especially low when participants perceived themselves to be more conservative than their close others (a₃ = -0.18, p < .001), or more closely aligned with the Republican Party than their close others (a₃ = -0.12, p = .005), compared with mismatches in the opposite direction. These directional mismatch effects were not significant for conflict.

For conflict, where politically similar participants and close others fell on the spectrum also mattered. Specifically, pairs who are both more conservative or both closer to the Republican party had higher conflict than pairs who are both more liberal (a₁ = 0.03, p = .049) or both closer to the Democratic party (a₁ = 0.06, p = .002).

Finally, for ideology predicting satisfaction, pairs who shared more extreme ideologies (whether conservative or liberal) had higher satisfaction than pairs who share moderate ideologies (i.e., neither conservative or liberal) (a₂ = 0.04, p = .006); this pattern was not significant for party alignment or for conflict.

Logistic Regression Models Predicting Breakups

Across T1-T8, 83 participants (8.1%) experienced at least one breakup with a partner or close other. Greater perceived political dissimilarity predicted higher odds of breakup, b = 0.17, z = 2.46, p = .014, OR = 1.19, 95% CI [1.03, 1.36]. Each one-unit increase in perceived political dissimilarity was associated with a 19% increase in the odds of breakup up until T8.

Table S5. Deviations from Pre-registration (Template from Willroth & Atherton, 2024) Deviations # Details Original Wording Deviation Description Reader Impact 1 Type Analysis The pre-registration states that we would use data from T0 to T8 (i.e., outcomes measured at T1-T8).

However, an extra follow-up survey was administered after the pre-registration was posted (T9). Including this additional timepoint is a strength by adding statistical power and expanding the time period in which we measured our outcome variables.

Instead of using outcome variables measured from T1 to T8, we will additionally include T9 and use T1-T9 data instead. Breakup, which was a participant-level variable (i.e., either broke up or not throughout the study) will also be expanded to include whether participants broke up at T9 (i.e., previously only covering the T0-T8 time period), which helped increase its base rate (i.e., from 8.1% to 9.3%).

There is largely no impact on the interpretation of the study or results. We included all timepoints available to maximize statistical power and report main results using data from T0-T9. The same set of analyses were also conducted using data from T0-T8 (pre-registered) and those results will be reported in the supplement.

Reason New Knowledge Timing After data access 2 Type Analysis The pre-registration states that we would run 72 moderation tests in total (based on 12 moderators). This was a typo given that we in fact had 13 moderators, which would result in 78 moderation tests in total.

We will run 78 moderation tests and report all models tested (i.e., apply the same FDR procedure as preregistered). The extra moderator was listed in the preregistration, but not counted when calculating the number of moderation tests.

There is no impact on the interpretation of the study or results. We will examine 13 moderators listed in the preregistration and apply FDR procedure to all tests as we originally planned.

Reason Typo/Error Timing After data access Note. Choose one characteristic for each dropdown menu. If more than one type or reason apply to the same deviation, then replace the dropdown menu with details. Provide one deviation per row, and add or delete rows as needed.

Figure Does Political Dissimilarity Predict Relationship Quality?

Additional Information about Response Surface Analysis Results

Dissimilarity effects were examined based on the curvature along the Line of Incongruence (LOIC), represented by the surface parameter a₄ (Table X). A significant a₄ indicates that relationship quality increases or decreases as participants perceive their own or their close other's political leanings to be more dissimilar. However, all response surface parameters need to be interpreted in tandem (Humberg et al., 2019; Schönbrodt et al., 2018).

A strict dissimilarity pattern is supported when only a₄ is significant and the other four parameters are all non-significant. A broad dissimilarity pattern is supported when a₄ is significant, and a3 and a5 are not significantly different from zero, and a2 and a1 may also be significant. These additional parameters suggest that dissimilarity effects may additionally depend on the direction of the mismatch, reflected by a3 (e.g., whether participants perceive themselves to be more conservative or liberal than their close others), or where participants and their close others match along the political scale, reflected by a1 and a2 (e.g., whether participants perceive both themselves and their close others to be similarly more liberal vs.

more conservative, and more extreme vs. moderate).

Additional Information about Breakup Rates by Difference Score between Self and Other Ideology/Party Alignment

Table S3. Proportions of People at Different levels of Political Dissimilarity Ideological Dissimilarity Party Alignment Dissimilarity Political dissimilarity size # of people with this dissimilarity size (and %) # of people who broke up with this dissimilarity size Breakup rate for those with this dissimilarity size # of people with this dissimilarity size (and %) # of people who broke up with this dissimilarity size Breakup rate for those with this dissimilarity size 0 point difference n=506 (49%) n=38 8% n=306 (30%) n=22 7% Additional Information on Moderators People perceived lower dissimilarity in romantic relationships than in non-romantic relationships, F(1, 1028) = 32.71, p < .001. Specifically, participants perceived romantic partners as significantly less dissimilar than family members (Cohen's d = -0.32, 95% CI [-0.48, -0.16], p < .001) and friends/acquaintances (d = -0.54, 95% CI [-0.77, -0.32], p < .001). Romantic partners were also marginally less politically dissimilar than best friends (d = -0.28, 95% CI [-0.50, -0.06], Tukey-adjusted p = .053). No other pairwise differences were significant. People also perceive greater importance of being similar to a romantic partner (M = 3.55) than a non-romantic close other (M = 2.35), t(797.04) = 9.60, p < .001, d = 0.62, 95% CI [0.50, 0.75], and reported having more frequent political conversations in romantic relationships (M = 3.59) than in non-romantic relationships (M = 2.30), t(832.67) = 12.12, p < .001, d = 0.78, 95% CI [0.65, 0.91]. Romantic relationships were rated as somewhat closer (M = 5.22) than non-romantic relationships (M = 4.98), t(838.73) = 3.25, p = .001, d = 0.21, 95% CI [0.08, 0.33]. Findings were similar for relationship conflict. Participants who perceived their relationships to be more politically dissimilar at T0 reported greater conflict across T1-T8, b = 0.05, t(955) = 2.66, p = .008, 95% CI [0.01, 0.09]. This association remained significant when controlling for concurrent well-being, b = 0.04, t(955) = 2.16, p = .031, and when predicting T2-T8 conflict while controlling for T1 conflict, b = 0.03, t(823) = 1.99, p = .048.

Thus, perceived political dissimilarity was associated with poorer relationship quality even when accounting for participants' ongoing well-being and earlier relationship quality.

Multilevel Response Surface Analysis Using Self and Other Ratings

Second, we examined political dissimilarity as incongruence between participants' own ideology or party alignment and their perceived close other's ideology or party alignment using multilevel Response Surface Analysis (RSA; Nestler et al., 2019).

Dissimilarity effects.

Changes in Perceived Importance of Political Similarity and Time SpentDiscussing Politics Across Time (Whole Sample)Even though Republicans tended to see being politically similar to their close other as less important (b = -0.35, p = .002), they spent more time discussing politics with their close other every week (b = 0.22, p = .01).ran multilevel models to examine whether participants' continuous ratings of perceived political dissimilarity at baseline (T0) predicted relationship satisfaction and conflict across subsequent timepoints (T1-T8). Participants who perceived their relationships to be more politically dissimilar at T0 reported lower relationship satisfaction across the study (T0-T8), b = -0.23, t(955) = -8.91, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.28, -0.18]. This association remained significant when controlling for participants' concurrent well-being at each timepoint, b = -0.21, t(955) = -8.66, p < .001, and when predicting T2-T8 satisfaction while controlling for T1 satisfaction, b = -0.10, t(823) = -5.59, p < .001.

We note that 25 people selected "other" rather than one of the provided options and were thus automatically sent to the version of the survey that asked questions about a "close other". These people also provided an openended response that we coded as not being in a current romantic relationship (e.g., divorced, separated, widowed). An additional three people selected "Other" and indicated a romantic partner in their open-ended response. According to the survey flow logic, these people also answered questions about a close other throughout the surveys.

Individual relationship lengths were validated against participant age, with one extreme baseline outlier of 116 years excluded as invalid, and two people indicating their relationship was more than two years longer than their age. These three participants were excluded from analyses involving relationship length.
